# LECTURE 20 — Stereo Geometry — Part V

making the estimate legal — enforcing rank-2 with SVD · the essential-matrix constraint · the 7-point method

🧠 the big picture, first

The 8-point algorithm gives a matrix — but not yet a valid fundamental matrix, because it ignored $\det F=0$. This lecture **repairs** the estimate with an **SVD trick**, does the same (plus an extra constraint) for $E$, and shows the minimal **7-point** method.

Key idea: any messy estimate can be **projected** to the nearest matrix that obeys the constraint. 🔧

**📎 stereo essentials to keep on hand (from Week 4 / L16–19)**
This week's assignment reviews the whole stereo topic, so keep these Week-4 results handy:

- **Skew matrix** for a cross product: $[\mathbf e]_\times=\begin{bmatrix}0&-e_3&e_2\\ e_3&0&-e_1\\ -e_2&e_1&0\end{bmatrix}$, so $\mathbf e\times\mathbf x=[\mathbf e]_\times\mathbf x$.

- **$F$ and $E$:** $F=[\mathbf e']_\times K'RK^{-1}$; calibrated $E=[\mathbf t]_\times R$; convert with $\ E=K'^{T}F\,K\ $ and $\ F=K'^{-T}E\,K^{-1}$.

- **Pure translation** ($R=I$, same $K$): $H_\infty=I$, so $F=[\mathbf e']_\times$. Translation along $x$ ⇒ $\mathbf e'=(1,0,0)^{T}$ (along $y$ ⇒ $(0,1,0)^{T}$) — epipole **at infinity**, epipolar lines **parallel**, and $y=y'$.

- **Depth from disparity** (rectified/translation rig, baseline $B=t_x$): $\ \text{disparity}=x-x'=\tfrac{fB}{Z}\ \Rightarrow\ Z=\tfrac{fB}{x-x'}$.

- **Back-projected ray** two ways: $\tilde{\mathbf X}(\lambda)=\tilde{\mathbf C}+\lambda M^{-1}\mathbf x$, equivalently $\mathbf X(\lambda)=P^{+}\mathbf x+\lambda\mathbf C$ (the point $P^{+}\mathbf x$ at $\lambda=0$ and the camera centre $\mathbf C$ as $\lambda\to\infty$).

## 1.  recap — normalize first

As in Lecture 19: map each image's coordinates to a small, centred range before estimating, then de-normalize $F=T'^{T}\tilde F T$. Note $T$ must be **upper-triangular** — it scales and translates the centre to the origin (a pure $\operatorname{diag}$ can only scale, not shift):

$$ T=\begin{bmatrix}s&0&-s\,c_x\\ 0&s&-s\,c_y\\ 0&0&1\end{bmatrix},\quad (c_x,c_y)=\text{image centre} $$

This is what makes the least-squares solution numerically trustworthy.

## 2.  enforcing the singularity constraint

A true $F$ has $\operatorname{rank}F=2$ (so $\det F=0$). The estimate usually comes out full-rank. Fix it with the **Singular Value Decomposition** $F=U\Sigma V^{T}$, where $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\sigma_3)$ with $\sigma_1\ge\sigma_2\ge\sigma_3$:

closest rank-2 matrix

$$ F'=U\,\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\sigma_2,0)\,V^{T} $$

**read it as:** $F$ is a sum of 3 rank-1 pieces weighted by $\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\sigma_3$. **Zeroing the smallest** $\sigma_3$ drops the least-important piece → a rank-2 matrix, and it's the **closest** one to $F$ in Frobenius (element-wise L2) norm.

**⚠️ why this actually matters (geometry)**
If $F$ isn't singular, the epipolar lines you draw **don't meet** at a single epipole — geometrically impossible. Enforcing rank-2 guarantees all epipolar lines converge to the true epipole. (Classic Hartley–Zisserman illustration.)

## 3.  the essential matrix's extra constraint

$E$ has only **5 DOF**, so beyond $\det E=0$ it needs its **two non-zero singular values to be equal**. Enforce it by averaging them:

$$ E'=U\,\operatorname{diag}\!\Big(\tfrac{\sigma_1+\sigma_2}{2},\ \tfrac{\sigma_1+\sigma_2}{2},\ 0\Big)V^{T} $$

(You estimate $E$ exactly like $F$ but on calibrated/normalized coordinates, then apply this projection.)

## 4.  the 7-point algorithm (minimum data)

Since $F$ has 7 DOF, **7 correspondences** suffice. Now $A$ is $7\times9$ (rank 7), so its null-space is **2-dimensional** — SVD gives two null vectors $\mathbf f_1,\mathbf f_2$ (i.e. matrices $F_1,F_2$). The answer is some combination:

$$ F=F_1+\lambda F_2 $$

Pin down $\lambda$ with the rank constraint $\det(F_1+\lambda F_2)=0$ — a **cubic** in $\lambda$, giving **1 or 3 real** solutions (so up to three candidate fundamental matrices).

**🧷 aside — rank-forcing parametrizations**
You can also build in the rank-2 constraint by parametrizing $F$ so its third column is a fixed linear combination of the first two (making one epipole $(\alpha,\beta,-1)^{T}$ explicit) — then $\det F=0$ holds automatically. An over-parametrized form is $F=[\mathbf t]_\times M$ with $\mathbf t$ the epipole.

### project the estimate onto the constraint: zero the smallest singular value → the nearest rank-2 $F$.

SVD $F=U\Sigma V^{T}$; $F'=U\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\sigma_2,0)V^{T}$; for $E$ also equalize $\sigma_1,\sigma_2$; 7 points ⇒ $F_1+\lambda F_2$, solve the cubic $\det=0$.

### Self-check quiz

1. The 8-point estimate is full-rank. How do you make it a valid (rank-2) fundamental matrix?

→ Answer: SVD $F=U\Sigma V^{T}$, set the smallest singular value to 0: $F'=U\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\sigma_2,0)V^{T}$ — the closest rank-2 matrix

2. Geometrically, what goes wrong if $F$ is not enforced to be singular?

→ Answer: the epipolar lines fail to converge to a single epipole

3. What extra constraint does the essential matrix satisfy, and how is it enforced?

→ Answer: its two non-zero singular values must be equal; set both to their average via SVD

4. Why does the 7-point method yield up to three fundamental matrices?

→ Answer: the null-space is 2-D ($F=F_1+\lambda F_2$); enforcing $\det=0$ is a cubic in $\lambda$ with 1 or 3 real roots

— end of Lecture 20 notes ✏️

# LECTURE 21 — Stereo Geometry — Part VI

cameras from F (up to a projective ambiguity) · cameras from E · triangulation → 3-D structure

🧠 the big picture, first

Given $F$ (or $E$) from image matches, can we recover the **cameras** and then the **3-D scene**? Yes — but with a twist: from $F$ alone the reconstruction is only fixed up to an unknown projective transformation. With calibration ($E$) you get true metric shape.

## 1.  $F$ does not fix the cameras (projective ambiguity)

A camera pair $(P,P')$ gives a unique $F$. But the reverse fails: for **any** invertible $4\times4$ $H$, the pair $(PH,\,P'H)$ gives the **same** $F$ — just re-coordinatize the world by $H^{-1}$.

$$ (P,P')\ \to\ F\quad\text{but}\quad (PH,\,P'H)\ \to\ \text{the same }F $$

**read it as:** $F$ depends only on image coordinates, so it's blind to how you set up world coordinates — you can slide/warp the whole rig and $F$ is unchanged. A neat DOF check: $2\times11-\underbrace{15}_{\text{DOF of the }4\times4\,H}=7=$ DOF of $F$ (the $4\times4$ world homography $H$ has $16-1=15$ DOF). ✅ Consequence: from $F$ you get a **projective reconstruction**, correct up to an overall projective transform.

## 2.  compatibility test

Is a given $F$ consistent with a given $(P,P')$? Check one property — no need to recompute $F$:

compatibility condition

$$ P'^{T} F\,P \ \text{is skew-symmetric} $$

(A skew-symmetric $S$ satisfies $S^{T}=-S$, zero diagonal, and $\mathbf X^{T}S\mathbf X=0$ for all $\mathbf X$ — which is exactly the epipolar constraint pushed back to 3-D.) In a problem: compute $P'^{T}FP$; if it isn't skew-symmetric, they're incompatible.

## 3.  a canonical camera pair from $F$

Pick the reference as $P=[\,I\mid\mathbf 0\,]$; then one valid second camera (using the right epipole $\mathbf e'$, where $F^{T}\mathbf e'=\mathbf 0$) is:

$$ P=[\,I\mid\mathbf 0\,],\qquad P'=\big[\,[\mathbf e']_\times F \ \big|\ \mathbf e'\,\big] $$

The full family is $P'=\big[\,[\mathbf e']_\times F+\mathbf e'\mathbf v^{T}\ \big|\ k\,\mathbf e'\,\big]$ for any 3-vector $\mathbf v$ and scalar $k$ — that's the projective ambiguity made explicit.

## 4.  cameras from the essential matrix (metric)

With calibration, $E=[\mathbf t]_\times R$ decomposes cleanly. SVD $E=U\operatorname{diag}(1,1,0)V^{T}$ and use the fixed matrix $W=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1&0\\1&0&0\\0&0&1\end{bmatrix}$:

$$ R=UWV^{T}\ \text{or}\ UW^{T}V^{T},\qquad \mathbf t=\pm\,\mathbf u_3 \ (\text{last column of }U) $$

**🎯 four candidates, one winner (cheirality)**
The two choices of $R$ times the two signs of $\mathbf t$ give **4 possible** second cameras $P'$. Only **one** places the reconstructed point **in front of both cameras** (positive depth) — test one point's cheirality and pick that configuration.

## 5.  triangulation — recovering the 3-D point

Given matches $\mathbf x\leftrightarrow\mathbf x'$ and cameras $P,P'$, find the scene point $X$ (this is **structure recovery**). Three ways:
| method | idea | note |
| **Geometric midpoint** | back-project both rays; take the midpoint of their common perpendicular | simple; not projective-invariant |
| **Reprojection error** | minimise $\lVert\mathbf x-P\hat X\rVert^2+\lVert\mathbf x'-P'\hat X\rVert^2$ (non-linear) | best; projective-invariant |
| **Linear (DLT)** | $\mathbf x\times PX=0$ & $\mathbf x'\times P'X=0$ | fast; solves $AX=0$ |

The **linear** one is the DLT again: each view's $\mathbf x\times PX=\mathbf 0$ gives **2** independent equations (rows of $P$), so two views give **4 equations** in the **3** unknowns of $X$. Stack into $AX=\mathbf 0$ and take the smallest right-singular vector (SVD):

$$ A X=\mathbf 0,\qquad \min\lVert AX\rVert\ \text{s.t.}\ \lVert X\rVert=1 $$

It generalises to $N$ views (each adds 2 equations) — e.g. a 3-camera rig gives 6 equations for the same 3 unknowns.

### from $F$ you get cameras only up to a projective warp; from $E$ (calibrated) you get metric cameras — then triangulate.

compatibility: $P'^{T}FP$ skew; canonical $P'=[[\mathbf e']_\times F\mid\mathbf e']$; $E$→4 poses, pick by cheirality; triangulate via $AX=0$.

### Self-check quiz

1. Why can't you recover unique cameras from $F$ alone?

→ Answer: $(PH,P'H)$ gives the same $F$ for any invertible $4\times4$ $H$ — reconstruction is fixed only up to a projective transform

2. How do you check whether a fundamental matrix is compatible with a camera pair $(P,P')$?

→ Answer: verify that $P'^{T}FP$ is skew-symmetric

3. Decomposing $E$ gives four candidate second cameras. How do you choose the right one?

→ Answer: cheirality — pick the one for which a reconstructed point is in front of both cameras (positive depth)

4. In linear triangulation, how many equations does each view contribute and why?

→ Answer: 2 independent equations from $\mathbf x\times PX=0$ (the third is dependent); two views → 4 equations for 3 unknowns

— end of Lecture 21 notes ✏️

# LECTURE 22 — Stereo Geometry — Part VII

triangulation worked out · reconstructing a 3-D line · the plane-induced homography

🧠 the big picture, first

We put triangulation to work on a concrete example, reconstruct a whole **line** (not just points), and meet a beautiful fact: whenever the scene points lie on a **plane**, the two images are related by a single homography — the plane-induced homography, the bridge between $F$ and $H$.

## 1.  worked example — triangulate a scene point

**given $P,P'$ and matches $(0,3.5)\leftrightarrow(\tfrac23,-\tfrac13)$, find $X$ (geometric method)**

1. Camera centres: $\tilde{\mathbf C}=-M^{-1}\mathbf m$, $\tilde{\mathbf C}'=-M'^{-1}\mathbf m'$.

2. Ray directions: $\mathbf d=M^{-1}\mathbf x$, $\mathbf d'=M'^{-1}\mathbf x'$.

3. The two rays $\tilde{\mathbf C}+s\,\mathbf d$ and $\tilde{\mathbf C}'+t\,\mathbf d'$ won't exactly meet (noise). The **common-perpendicular** direction is $\mathbf d\times\mathbf d'$.

4. Solve the two linear equations that force the segment joining the rays to be perpendicular to both $\mathbf d$ and $\mathbf d'$ → get $s,t$; the scene point is the **midpoint** of that shortest segment. ✅

## 2.  reconstructing a 3-D line

A 3-D line $L$ images to lines $\mathbf l$ (left) and $\mathbf l'$ (right). Each image line + its camera centre spans a 3-D plane; the 3-D line is where those two planes meet:

$$ \boldsymbol\pi=P^{T}\mathbf l,\qquad \boldsymbol\pi'=P'^{T}\mathbf l',\qquad L=\boldsymbol\pi\cap\boldsymbol\pi' $$

(Back-project each image line to its plane, intersect the two planes — done.)

## 3.  the plane-induced homography

If all the scene points lie on one plane $\boldsymbol\pi$ (normal direction $\mathbf v$), then their left and right images are related by a single homography $H$ ($\mathbf x'=H\mathbf x$):

plane-induced homography

$$ H=A-\mathbf a\,\mathbf v^{T} $$

$A=$ left $3\times3$ block of $P'$, $\ \mathbf a=$ its 4th column

**where it comes from:** back-project $\mathbf x$; the depth $\rho=-\mathbf v^{T}\mathbf x$ is fixed by "point lies on $\boldsymbol\pi$"; substitute into $\mathbf x'=P'X$ to get $\mathbf x'=(A-\mathbf a\mathbf v^{T})\mathbf x$. Different plane ⇒ different $H$.

## 4.  homography ↔ fundamental matrix

**🔗 the links**

- $F$ factors through any plane-induced $H$: $\ F=[\mathbf e']_\times H$, and $\ \mathbf e'=H\mathbf e$.

- **Compatibility:** $H$ is a valid plane-induced homography for $F$ iff $H^{T}F$ is **skew-symmetric** (proof: $\mathbf x'^{T}F\mathbf x=0$ with $\mathbf x'=H\mathbf x$).

- The mapped point lies on the epipolar line: $(H\mathbf x)^{T}F\mathbf x=0$.

**$F$ from 6 points, 4 of them coplanar**

1. From the 4 coplanar matches, compute the plane-induced homography $H$ (4 correspondences).

2. For the two off-plane points, form epipolar lines $\mathbf l_1=(H\mathbf x_5)\times\mathbf x_5'$, $\mathbf l_2=(H\mathbf x_6)\times\mathbf x_6'$.

3. Right epipole $\mathbf e'=\mathbf l_1\times\mathbf l_2$; then $\ F=[\mathbf e']_\times H$. ✅

## 5.  the homography at infinity

Take the inducing plane to be the plane at infinity ($d\to\infty$). The plane-induced homography becomes the **infinite homography**:

$$ H_\infty=K'RK^{-1}=M'M^{-1} $$

It **maps vanishing points** between the two images (images of points at infinity). You can compute it from **3 non-collinear vanishing-point correspondences given $F$**, or from 4 vanishing-point pairs. This is the same $H_\infty$ that appeared in $F=[\mathbf e']_\times H_\infty$.

### points on a plane ⇒ one homography $H=A-\mathbf a\mathbf v^{T}$; and $F=[\mathbf e']_\times H$ ties homographies to the epipolar map.

triangulate points via common perpendicular; a 3-D line = $P^{T}\mathbf l \cap P'^{T}\mathbf l'$; $H_\infty=M'M^{-1}$ maps vanishing points.

### Self-check quiz

1. In geometric triangulation, why don't the two back-projected rays exactly intersect, and what's the fix?

→ Answer: measurement noise; take the midpoint of their common-perpendicular (shortest) segment

2. How do you reconstruct a 3-D line from its two images?

→ Answer: intersect the planes $\boldsymbol\pi=P^{T}\mathbf l$ and $\boldsymbol\pi'=P'^{T}\mathbf l'$

3. When do two stereo images relate by a single homography, and what's its form?

→ Answer: when the scene points are coplanar; $H=A-\mathbf a\mathbf v^{T}$ (plane-induced homography)

4. How is a plane-induced homography linked to the fundamental matrix?

→ Answer: $F=[\mathbf e']_\times H$ (and $H^{T}F$ is skew-symmetric for a compatible $H$)

— end of Lecture 22 notes ✏️

# LECTURE 23 — Stereo Geometry — Part VIII

affine epipolar geometry · the affine fundamental matrix · the grand stereo summary

🧠 the big picture, first

We finish stereo with its **simplest** special case — two **affine** cameras — where the fundamental matrix nearly empties out and singularity comes for free. Then a one-page recap of the entire two-view module.

## 1.  affine epipolar geometry

Recall an **affine camera** has its centre at infinity → **parallel** projection rays. So in an affine stereo pair:

**📏 what goes parallel**

- all **projection rays are parallel**, so all **epipolar lines are parallel** (and epipolar planes too);

- parallel epipolar lines meet at infinity ⇒ the **epipoles lie at infinity**: $\mathbf e=(e_1,e_2,0)^{T}$.

## 2.  the affine fundamental matrix

Because the epipoles are at infinity, the top-left $2\times2$ block of $F$ vanishes — only **5 entries** survive:

affine fundamental matrix

$$ F_A=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&a\\0&0&b\\c&d&e\end{bmatrix} $$

**✅ why this is so convenient**

- **4 DOF** (5 non-zero entries − scale) ⇒ only **4 point correspondences** to estimate (vs 8/7 for the full $F$).

- **Singularity is automatic**: with that zero block $\det F_A=0$ by construction — no SVD repair needed.

- Epipoles read straight off (both at infinity): the **right** epipole solves $F_A\mathbf e=\mathbf 0\Rightarrow\mathbf e=(d,-c,0)^{T}$ (from the last-row entries $c,d$); the **left** epipole solves $\mathbf e'^{T}F_A=\mathbf 0\Rightarrow\mathbf e'=(-b,a,0)^{T}$ (from the last-column entries $a,b$).

Estimate it by the same linear (DLT) setup on epipolar-constraint equations, or compute an affine plane-induced homography (needs 3 points) and read epipoles off the epipolar-line directions.

## 3.  the whole stereo module on one page

**📌 two-view geometry cheat-sheet**
| concept | the key fact |
| epipolar geometry | $X$, both centres & both image points are coplanar; matches lie on epipolar lines through the epipoles |
| fundamental matrix $F$ | $3\times3$, rank-2, $\det F=0$, **7 DOF**; $\mathbf x'^{T}F\mathbf x=0$; $\mathbf l'=F\mathbf x$; $F\mathbf e=\mathbf 0$, $\mathbf e'^{T}F=\mathbf 0$; swap cameras → $F^{T}$ |
| compute $F$ | from cameras $F=[\mathbf m]_\times M$ (or $[\mathbf e']_\times H_\infty$, $H_\infty=M'M^{-1}$) |
| estimate $F$ | normalized 8-point (or minimal 7-point); enforce rank-2 by zeroing the smallest singular value |
| essential matrix $E$ | calibrated: $E=[\mathbf t]_\times R$, 5 DOF, two equal singular values (third 0) |
| cameras from $F$ | only up to a projective $4\times4$ $H$; canonical $P'=[[\mathbf e']_\times F\mid\mathbf e']$ |
| cameras from $E$ | SVD → 4 poses, pick by cheirality (point in front of both) |
| triangulation | midpoint / reprojection-error / linear DLT ($AX=\mathbf 0$) → 3-D structure |
| plane-induced $H$ | coplanar scene ⇒ $H=A-\mathbf a\mathbf v^{T}$; $F=[\mathbf e']_\times H$ |
| affine stereo | parallel epipolar lines; $F_A$ has 4 DOF, auto-singular, 4 points |

### affine stereo = the easy case: parallel epipolar lines, a 4-DOF $F_A$ that's singular by construction.

and the module in one line: epipolar constraint $\mathbf x'^{T}F\mathbf x=0$ → estimate $F$/$E$ → recover cameras → triangulate structure.

### Self-check quiz

1. In an affine stereo pair, what do the epipolar lines and epipoles look like?

→ Answer: all epipolar lines are parallel; the epipoles are at infinity $(e_1,e_2,0)^{T}$

2. How many DOF does the affine fundamental matrix have, and why is its singularity free?

→ Answer: 4 DOF; the top-left $2\times2$ block is zero, so $\det F_A=0$ automatically

3. State the single most important equation of two-view geometry.

→ Answer: the epipolar constraint $\mathbf x'^{T}F\mathbf x=0$

4. What is the full pipeline from two images to 3-D structure?

→ Answer: match points → estimate $F$ (or $E$) → recover camera pair → triangulate the scene points

— end of Lecture 23 · end of Week 5 ✏️🎉

# LECTURE 24 — Feature Detection & Description — Part I

why corners · the SSD window measure · the structure matrix · the Harris corner detector

🧠 the big picture, first

Every earlier topic assumed we already had matched points across images. Now we **find them automatically**. The trick: pick points that are distinctive in every direction — and those turn out to be **corners**. Slide a little window; if the image changes no matter which way you nudge it, you've found a corner.

## 1.  the matching problem

Given two views of a scene, we want **corresponding points**. Three stages:

**🧩 detect → describe → match**

1. **Detect** repeatable landmark points (this lecture);

2. **Describe** each with a distinctive signature of its neighbourhood;

3. **Match** descriptors across images.

All three must survive transformations — translation, rotation, scale, illumination.

## 2.  flat vs edge vs corner

Take a small window and shift it a little in various directions; watch how much the intensities change:
| region | shift the window… | change |
| **flat** | any direction | ≈ no change |
| **edge** | along the edge / across it | no change / big change |
| **corner** | **any** direction | **big change every way** ✅ |

## 3.  the change measure & the structure matrix

Quantify "how much it changes" when we shift the window by $(u,v)$ with the **sum of squared differences**:

$$ E(u,v)=\sum_{(x,y)\in W}\big[\,I(x+u,\,y+v)-I(x,y)\,\big]^2 $$

For small shifts, first-order Taylor $I(x+u,y+v)\approx I+I_x u+I_y v$ turns $E$ into a neat quadratic form:

the structure (second-moment) matrix

$$ E(u,v)\approx\begin{bmatrix}u&v\end{bmatrix}H\begin{bmatrix}u\\v\end{bmatrix},\quad H=\sum_{W}\begin{bmatrix}I_x^2 & I_xI_y\\ I_xI_y & I_y^2\end{bmatrix} $$

**⚠️ average of squares, not square of average**
$H$ sums the **squared**/product gradients over the window. If you instead squared the averaged gradient, $\det H$ would be $0$ everywhere — and corner detection would fail.

## 4.  eigen-analysis — reading $H$

The eigenvectors of $H$ are the directions of largest and smallest change; the eigenvalues $\lambda_+\ge\lambda_-$ are the amounts of change:

#### the (λ₊, λ₋) map

[diagram text: λ₋ | λ₊ | flat | edge | CORNER]

flat: both small · edge: one large, one small · corner: **both large**.

**read it as:** a corner needs the **smaller** eigenvalue $\lambda_-$ to be large — that guarantees a big change even in the least-changing direction. So the corner score is $\min$ over all shift directions $=\lambda_-$.

### see it on a chessboard — the whole point of $\lambda_-$

Run the eigen-analysis at every pixel of a chessboard and map the eigenvalues. This is the figure that makes the idea click:

input: a chessboard
**λ₊** — bright on **edges AND cornersλ₋** — bright on **ONLY the corners**

Uniform squares → both eigenvalues ≈ 0 (black). Edges light up $\lambda_+$ but not $\lambda_-$. Only the **corners** light up $\lambda_-$ — which is exactly why $\lambda_-$ (not $\lambda_+$) is the corner score. (figure adapted from the course slides)

## 5.  the detection algorithm

1. Compute image gradients $I_x,I_y$ everywhere.

2. At each pixel, build $H$ from windowed sums of $I_x^2,\,I_y^2,\,I_xI_y$.

3. Compute the eigenvalues; keep points where $\lambda_-$ exceeds a threshold.

4. Take the **local maxima** of the response → precise corner locations. 📍

(On a chessboard: $\lambda_+$ lights up edges and corners, but $\lambda_-$ lights up **only** the corners.)

## 6.  the Harris measure (skip the square roots)

Computing eigenvalues needs a square root. **Harris** uses a cheaper response built from the determinant and trace instead:

Harris corner response

$$ f=\frac{\det H}{\operatorname{tr}H}=\frac{\lambda_+\lambda_-}{\lambda_++\lambda_-} $$

$\det H=\lambda_+\lambda_-$ (product), $\operatorname{tr}H=\lambda_++\lambda_-$ (sum) — no eigen-decomposition, no roots. Both eigenvalues large ⇒ $f$ large. Compute $f$ everywhere, take local maxima → the famous **Harris corner detector**.

**📎 exam note — which "Harris" is this?**
The measure taught here, $f=\dfrac{\det H}{\operatorname{tr}H}=\dfrac{\lambda_+\lambda_-}{\lambda_++\lambda_-}$, is the **harmonic mean** of the eigenvalues (the Noble/Brown variant). The **original Harris–Stephens** response is $R=\det H-k(\operatorname{tr}H)^2$ (with $k\approx0.04$–$0.06$), and **Shi–Tomasi** uses $\min(\lambda_+,\lambda_-)=\lambda_-$ directly. All three fire on corners; know all three names for the exam.

### corners = points where the windowed gradient matrix $H$ has **two large eigenvalues**.

$E(u,v)\approx[u\,v]H[u\,v]^T$; corner score = $\lambda_-$; Harris uses $f=\det H/\operatorname{tr}H$ (no roots) + local maxima.

### Self-check quiz

1. Why are corners better feature points than edges or flat regions?

→ Answer: a small window shift changes the intensity in every direction only at a corner — it's distinctive and localizable

2. What do the two eigenvalues of the structure matrix $H$ tell you?

→ Answer: the amount of intensity change in the most- and least-changing directions; a corner has both large

3. Which eigenvalue is the corner score, and why?

→ Answer: the smaller one $\lambda_-$ — it must be large so change is big even in the weakest direction

4. What is the Harris response and why is it used instead of eigenvalues?

→ Answer: $f=\det H/\operatorname{tr}H=\lambda_+\lambda_-/(\lambda_++\lambda_-)$ — it avoids square-root/eigen computation

— end of Lecture 24 notes ✏️

# LECTURE 25 — Feature Detection & Description — Part II

scale invariance · the Gaussian pyramid · LoG & DoG · SIFT keypoints (edge culling & orientation)

🧠 the big picture, first

Harris finds corners but is **scale-blind**: shrink the image and its corners shift. The fix is to search for features not just over position but over scale too — a 3-D search $(x,y,\sigma)$. This is the heart of **SIFT**.

## 1.  detection, description & the scale problem

Both **detection** and **description** must be invariant to transformation. Translation and rotation are relatively easy; **scale is the hard one** — a blob that looks like a corner up close may vanish when the image is shrunk. The idea: represent the image at **many scales** and find features that are strong at their own best scale.

## 2.  multi-scale — the Gaussian pyramid

Repeatedly blur (Gaussian) and down-sample the image → a stack of ever-coarser versions, the **Gaussian pyramid**. A feature is now a **local maximum in position AND scale** (a 3-D search).

**🎚️ octaves & the scale step $k$**
The scales are grouped into **octaves** — an octave spans a doubling of $\sigma$ (scale factor $2$). If one octave holds $s{+}1$ blurred images, there are $s$ intervals, so **adjacent scales differ by**

$$ k=2^{1/s} $$

e.g. $10$ images per octave $\Rightarrow s=9 \Rightarrow k=2^{1/9}\approx 1.080$. (At the top of each octave the image is down-sampled by 2 and the next octave begins.)

#### blur + subsample → a pyramid

[diagram text: σ, full res | 2σ, ½ res | 4σ, ¼ res]

Each level: convolve with a Gaussian, then subsample.

## 3.  the detector kernels — LoG & DoG

Start from the 2-D **Gaussian** (its width $\sigma$ = the "scale"):

$$ G(x,y,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^2}\exp\!\Big(-\frac{x^2+y^2}{2\sigma^2}\Big) $$

Two blob-detecting measures, searched for extrema over scale:

scale-normalized Laplacian (LoG)

$$ \nabla^2_{\text{norm}}=\sigma^2\big(G_{xx}+G_{yy}\big) $$

Difference of Gaussians (DoG)

$$ \text{DoG}=G(x,y,k\sigma)-G(x,y,\sigma) $$

The $\sigma^2$ makes the Laplacian **scale-invariant**. And DoG is a cheap stand-in for LoG — because $\dfrac{\partial G}{\partial\sigma}=\sigma\nabla^2G$, so

$$ G(x,y,k\sigma)-G(x,y,\sigma)\approx (k-1)\,\sigma^2\nabla^2G $$

i.e. DoG $\propto$ scale-normalized Laplacian (the constant $(k-1)$ doesn't move the extrema). So you just **subtract adjacent pyramid levels** — no derivatives to compute.

## 4.  SIFT keypoint detection

**🔺 the two popular detectors**

- **SIFT** (David Lowe): build a **DoG pyramid**, then find **extrema in the 3-D DoG scale-space** (compare each sample to its neighbours in position and the scales above/below).

- **Harris–Laplacian:** Harris corner response computed across a Laplacian pyramid; take maxima over scale & space.

## 5.  culling bad keypoints (edges out)

The raw extrema include many weak/edge points. As in Harris, corners are robust but edges aren't. Use the **Hessian of the DoG** $H=\begin{bmatrix}D_{xx}&D_{xy}\\D_{xy}&D_{yy}\end{bmatrix}$ — its eigenvalues are the **principal curvatures**. An edge has one large, one small (very different) → reject via the trace/determinant ratio:

edge-rejection test

$$ \frac{(\operatorname{tr}H)^2}{\det H}<\frac{(r+1)^2}{r}\quad(r=10) $$

Keep a keypoint only if its curvatures are comparable (ratio below threshold); reject if $\dfrac{(\operatorname{tr}H)^2}{\det H}$ exceeds it. (E.g. 832 keypoints → 729 after a gradient threshold → fewer after this ratio test.)

## 6.  orientation → rotation invariance

To be rotation-invariant, give each keypoint a **canonical orientation**: histogram the gradient directions in its neighbourhood (bins over $0$–$2\pi$), smooth it, and take the **peak**. Then describe everything relative to that direction.

**so a SIFT keypoint carries:position + scale + orientation.** (If the histogram has two strong peaks, the keypoint gets two descriptors.) Next lecture builds the descriptor itself.

**(a)** the image · **(b)** all detected keypoints (each arrow = position, scale & orientation) · **(c)** after the contrast/gradient threshold · **(d)** after the edge (curvature-ratio) test. Culling drops weak/edge points (e.g. 832 → 729 → fewer), keeping only robust corners. (from the course slides)

### search for features over position AND scale: DoG extrema in a Gaussian pyramid = SIFT keypoints.

DoG $\propto\sigma^2\nabla^2G$; cull edges by the Hessian ratio $(\operatorname{tr}H)^2/\det H$; a keypoint = position + scale + orientation.

### Self-check quiz

1. Why isn't the Harris detector scale-invariant, and what's the remedy?

→ Answer: its window is one fixed size; search over a multi-scale (Gaussian pyramid) representation — find maxima in position and scale

2. Why can SIFT use Difference-of-Gaussians instead of the Laplacian?

→ Answer: DoG $\approx (k-1)\sigma^2\nabla^2G$ — it's proportional to the scale-normalized Laplacian, but only needs subtracting adjacent blurred levels

3. How does SIFT throw out keypoints that lie on edges?

→ Answer: via the DoG Hessian's principal curvatures: reject if $(\operatorname{tr}H)^2/\det H$ exceeds $(r+1)^2/r$ (one curvature ≫ the other = edge)

4. What three things define a SIFT keypoint, and which gives rotation invariance?

→ Answer: position, scale, and orientation; the orientation (peak of the gradient histogram) provides rotation invariance

— end of Lecture 25 notes ✏️

# LECTURE 26 — Feature Detection & Description — Part III

the SIFT descriptor · SURF & integral images · fast binary methods: FAST, BRIEF, ORB

🧠 the big picture, first

We have keypoints (position + scale + orientation). Now give each a **signature** — a vector describing its neighbourhood — so we can match them. SIFT is the classic; then a family of **faster** descriptors (SURF, BRIEF, ORB) trade a little accuracy for real-time speed.

## 1.  the SIFT descriptor (128 numbers)

Around a keypoint, take a **$16\times16$ window**, first **rotated** to the keypoint's canonical orientation (that's what makes it rotation-invariant). Then:

**🧵 building the 128-D vector**

1. At each pixel compute the gradient; weak-magnitude ones are discarded.

2. Split the $16\times16$ window into a **$4\times4$ grid of cells** (16 cells).

3. Each cell → an **8-bin orientation histogram** (votes weighted by gradient magnitude) = an 8-vector.

4. Concatenate all 16 cells: $16\times8=\mathbf{128}$-dimensional descriptor. 🎯

**SIFT** (David Lowe, 1999) is robust to illumination and up to ~$60^\circ$ out-of-plane rotation.

## 2.  SURF — speed via integral images

**SURF** (2006) approximates SIFT's expensive Gaussian work with **box filters** (weights $\pm1$) evaluated using an **integral image**:

integral image

$$ I_\Sigma(x,y)=\sum_{i\le x}\sum_{j\le y} I(i,j) $$

**why it's fast:** once $I_\Sigma$ is built in **one scan**, the sum over **any** rectangle is just $I_\Sigma$ at 4 corners (add two, subtract two) — **3 additions, 4 look-ups**, independent of box size. So box-filter convolutions are $O(1)$ per pixel.

- **Detector:** local maxima of the **determinant of the Hessian**, $\det H_{\text{approx}}=D_{xx}D_{yy}-(w\,D_{xy})^2$ ($w\approx0.9$), with box-filter approximations of the 2nd derivatives across scales.

- **Descriptor:** Haar-wavelet responses in a $4\times4$ grid; each cell stores $(\sum d_x,\ \sum|d_x|,\ \sum d_y,\ \sum|d_y|)$ = 4 numbers → $16\times4=\mathbf{64}$-D.

## 3.  fast binary methods — FAST, BRIEF, ORB

**⚡ real-time detectors & descriptors**

- **FAST** (detector): look at 16 pixels on a circle around a candidate; if **12 consecutive** are all brighter (or all darker) than the centre → corner. A trained decision tree (darker/similar/brighter) speeds it further. Not rotation/scale invariant.

- **BRIEF** (descriptor): pick $n$ random point-pairs in the patch; each test $\tau=\mathbb 1[\,I(\mathbf p_i)binary string (128/256/512). Compared with Hamming distance — extremely fast. Not rotation invariant.

- **ORB** = **O**riented FAST + **R**otated BRIEF: adds orientation via the **intensity centroid** (intensity-weighted centre gives a direction), then "steers" BRIEF to that angle + uses a scale pyramid. → rotation & scale aware, still fast.

## 4.  the descriptor zoo, compared
| method | detector | descriptor | note |
| **SIFT** | DoG extrema | 128-D gradient histograms | gold standard; slow |
| **SURF** | Hessian det (box) | 64-D Haar responses | integral image → fast |
| **FAST/BRIEF** | 12-point circle test | binary string | fastest; no rot/scale |
| **ORB** | oriented FAST | steered/rotated BRIEF | fast + rotation/scale |

### a descriptor turns a keypoint's neighbourhood into a matchable vector — SIFT (128-D) for accuracy, ORB for speed.

SIFT = 4×4 cells × 8-bin gradient histograms; SURF uses integral images + box filters; BRIEF/ORB = binary strings via intensity tests.

### Self-check quiz

1. How does the 128-dimensional SIFT descriptor arise?

→ Answer: an orientation-corrected $16\times16$ window → $4\times4$ cells × 8-bin gradient-orientation histograms = $16\times8=128$

2. What makes SURF fast, and how does an integral image help?

→ Answer: box filters instead of Gaussians; an integral image gives any rectangle's sum in 3 add/4 look-ups regardless of size

3. What is the FAST corner test?

→ Answer: on 16 pixels around a circle, ≥12 consecutive brighter-or-darker than the centre ⇒ a corner

4. How does ORB make FAST+BRIEF rotation-invariant?

→ Answer: it computes an orientation from the intensity centroid and steers (rotates) the BRIEF sampling pattern to it

— end of Lecture 26 notes ✏️

# LECTURE 27 — Feature Detection & Description — Part IV

matching by distance · region descriptors: HOG · texture: LBP, co-occurrence matrix, Laws energy

🧠 the big picture, first

Once every keypoint is a vector, **matching** is just "find the nearest vector." Then we zoom out from points to **regions**: how to describe a whole patch or texture (HOG for shapes, LBP / co-occurrence / Laws for textures) — the descriptors behind object and material recognition.

## 1.  matching by distance

Two keypoints match if their descriptors are **close**. Measure closeness with a norm between feature vectors $\mathbf u,\mathbf v$:

$$ L_1=\sum_i|u_i-v_i|,\quad L_2=\sqrt{\sum_i (u_i-v_i)^2},\quad L_p=\Big(\sum_i |u_i-v_i|^p\Big)^{1/p} $$

($L_1$ = Manhattan, $L_2$ = Euclidean; both special cases of $L_p$.) The nearest descriptor is the candidate match. **Non-maximal suppression** then removes duplicate/overlapping detections greedily: keep the best-scoring window, suppress those too close, repeat.

## 2.  region descriptor — Histogram of Oriented Gradients (HOG)

**HOG** (2005) describes a whole patch by its gradient-orientation statistics — great for **shape** (famously, pedestrian detection):

**📐 HOG recipe (for a 64×128 patch)**

1. Compute gradients (no smoothing); for colour, use the channel with the largest gradient.

2. Divide into $16\times16$ **blocks** with 50% overlap → ~**105 blocks**; each block = $2\times2$ cells of $8\times8$.

3. Per cell: a **9-bin** orientation histogram, votes weighted by magnitude (interpolated, Gaussian-weighted).

4. Concatenate: $105\times4\times9=\mathbf{3780}$-D descriptor → feed a classifier (SVM / random forest).

## 3.  texture — first, what is it?

**Texture** is a **spatial arrangement of intensities/colours** in a region — a quantitative measure of that arrangement distinctly identifies a material (sky vs road vs foliage). The professor lists **four** texture techniques: **edge density & direction**, **LBP**, **co-occurrence matrix**, and **Laws energy**.

### method 1 — edge density & direction

Compute the gradient at every pixel in the region, then build **two normalized histograms** — one of gradient **magnitudes**, one of gradient **directions** (normalized so each histogram's area = 1, i.e. a probability density; typically a small number of bins, ~10). The concatenated pair is the texture descriptor; compare textures with an $L_1$/$L_2$ distance.

## 4.  texture descriptor — Local Binary Pattern (LBP)

Compare a central pixel $c$ to its 8 neighbours; each is a bit (1 if brighter). Read the 8 bits as a number:

local binary pattern

$$ \text{LBP}(c)=\sum_{i=0}^{7} b(i)\,2^{i},\qquad b(i)=\begin{cases}1 & I_i\ge I_c\\ 0 & \text{else}\end{cases} $$

So each pixel gets a value $0$–$255$; a **normalized histogram** over the region is the texture descriptor. It's invariant to monotonic illumination change, but **not rotation**.

**🔄 rotation-invariant & uniform LBP**

- Use a **circular** neighbourhood ($P$ points, radius $R$; interpolate off-grid points). For rotation invariance, take the **minimum** over all circular bit-shifts (rotating the image just rotates the string).

- **Uniform** patterns have $\le 2$ bit-transitions (e.g. 11100011); for $P=8$ there are 9 uniform patterns → a compact **10-bin** histogram (9 uniform + 1 "all others"). Add local **variance** for intensity contrast.

## 5.  texture descriptor — co-occurrence matrix

Count how often two grey levels appear at a fixed spatial offset $\mathbf t=(a,b)$: the $(x,y)$ entry is the number of pixel pairs with values $x$ and $y$ at that relation.

$$ C_{\mathbf t}(x,y)=\#\{\, \mathbf p:\ I(\mathbf p)=x\ \text{and}\ I(\mathbf p+\mathbf t)=y\,\} $$

**worked example — a 2-level 4×4 image, offset "one step right"**
Counting each ordered pair $(x,y)$ across the image gave the professor:

$$ C=\begin{bmatrix}4 & 2\\ 2 & 4\end{bmatrix} $$

i.e. $(0,0)$ occurred 4×, $(0,1)$ and $(1,0)$ twice each, $(1,1)$ 4×. Normalize (divide by the total) → a probability matrix; from it derive features like **correlation, contrast, energy, homogeneity**. Different offsets → different matrices → a richer texture signature.

## 6.  texture descriptor — Laws energy

**Laws' texture energy** builds masks from four 1-D filters — **L5** (level/average), **E5** (edge), **S5** (spot), **R5** (ripple):

- Take **outer products** of pairs (e.g. $\mathbf{E5}\,\mathbf{L5}^{T}$) → $5\times5$ 2-D masks; combine symmetric pairs down to **9 masks**.

- Convolve, then compute **energy** = sum of squared/absolute responses in a window (e.g. $15\times15$) → a **9-D** feature per pixel.

All these region descriptors feed the same downstream task: match to a labelled library or train a classifier (segmentation, object/material recognition).

### match points by nearest descriptor; describe regions by gradient/texture statistics (HOG, LBP, co-occurrence, Laws).

$L_1/L_2$ distances + non-max suppression; HOG = 9-bin gradient histograms; LBP = thresholded-neighbour bit string; co-occurrence counts grey-level pairs.

### Self-check quiz

1. Two SIFT descriptors — how do you decide if they match, and what removes duplicate detections?

→ Answer: nearest descriptor by an $L_1$/$L_2$ distance; non-maximal suppression removes overlapping duplicates

2. What does HOG capture and what is a classic application?

→ Answer: gradient-orientation histograms over a grid (shape); pedestrian detection

3. Write the LBP value formula and state one invariance it lacks.

→ Answer: $\text{LBP}=\sum_i b(i)2^i$ (bit = neighbour ≥ centre); it is not rotation-invariant (fixed by circular LBP + min-over-shifts)

4. What does a grey-level co-occurrence matrix count?

→ Answer: how often each pair of grey values $(x,y)$ occurs at a fixed spatial offset $\mathbf t$

— end of Lecture 27 notes ✏️

# LECTURE 28 — Feature Detection & Description — Part V

describing a whole image · Bag of Visual Words · VLAD · content-based image retrieval

🧠 the big picture, first

We've described points and regions; now the **whole image** in one vector — so you can **search by picture**. The trick borrows from text search: build a dictionary of "visual words" and describe any image by which visual words it contains.

## 1.  Bag of Visual Words (BoVW)

Text documents are described by which dictionary words they contain. Images have no ready-made dictionary — so we **build** one from local descriptors:

**📖 building & using the visual dictionary**

1. Over a **library** of images, extract keypoints (SIFT/SURF/FAST) and their descriptors → a huge pile of vectors.

2. **Cluster** them with **K-means** into $K$ groups; each cluster **mean** is a **visual word**. The $K$ words are the **codebook**.

3. For any image: describe each keypoint, assign it to its **nearest visual word**, and **count** → a $K$-bin **histogram**.

Every image becomes a fixed **$K$-dimensional** vector, regardless of how many keypoints it had. 🎯

## 2.  VLAD — remember the residuals

BoVW only counts words. **VLAD** (Vector of Locally Aggregated Descriptors) keeps **more**: for each visual word $C_i$, it sums the **differences** between the descriptors assigned to it and the centre:

VLAD aggregation

$$ \mathbf v_i=\!\!\sum_{\text{NN}(\mathbf x)=C_i}\!\!(\mathbf x-C_i) $$

Concatenate all $K$ residual vectors (each of the descriptor dimension $D$, e.g. $D=128$ for SIFT) → a $K\times D$ vector, then **L2-normalize**:

$$ \text{VLAD}=\frac{[\,\mathbf v_1;\mathbf v_2;\dots;\mathbf v_K\,]}{\big\lVert[\,\mathbf v_1;\dots;\mathbf v_K\,]\big\rVert} $$

It's higher-dimensional than BoVW (so $K$ is kept small), but more discriminative — it records where descriptors sit relative to each word, not just how many.

## 3.  application — content-based image retrieval

A global descriptor lets you **search by image**: describe every library image; given a query image, compute its descriptor and return the library images whose descriptors are **closest**, ranked.

**in practice:** "photograph this temple carving, find what it is." (The professor's examples: the chariot at Hampi's Vithala temple and the Bishnupur terracotta temples.) Occasional wrong matches happen because different images can land on similar feature vectors.

## 4.  look-ahead — matching & robust model fitting

These tools belong to the next topic (matching & model fitting) but are the natural sequel to descriptors — and show up in the assignment, so know them:
| tool | what it does |
| **RANSAC** | **Robust model fitting with outliers:** randomly sample a minimal set, fit a model, count inliers (points within a threshold), repeat, keep the model with the most inliers. Tolerates a large fraction of wrong matches — the standard way to fit $F$/$H$ from noisy correspondences. |
| **Hough transform** | **Voting-based line/shape fitting:** each point votes for all lines through it in parameter space; peaks in the accumulator = detected lines. Strong when there are **many** lines / much clutter. |
| **K-D tree** | **Fast nearest-neighbour** search for matching descriptors: a binary space-partition tree; average query $\approx O(\log n)$ (grows with dimension $d$). Speeds up "find the closest descriptor." |
| **KL divergence** | A **statistical distance** between two probability distributions $D_{KL}(P\Vert Q)=\sum_i P_i\log\tfrac{P_i}{Q_i}$ — used to compare histograms/distributions. **Not** a geometric line-fitting criterion (a common distractor). |

## 5.  the whole feature topic on one page

**📌 feature detection & description cheat-sheet**
| stage | methods |
| **detection** | Harris corner (here $f=\det M/\operatorname{tr}M$ on the structure matrix $M$; canonical Harris–Stephens is $\det M-k(\operatorname{tr}M)^2$); scale via Laplacian/DoG extrema (SIFT); Hessian-det (SURF); FAST |
| **keypoint = ** | position + scale + orientation (from the gradient histogram peak) |
| **point descriptors** | SIFT (128-D), SURF (64-D, integral image), BRIEF/ORB (binary) |
| **region descriptors** | HOG (gradient histograms, shape) |
| **texture descriptors** | edge density, LBP (+rotation-invariant/uniform), co-occurrence matrix, Laws energy |
| **global descriptors** | Bag of Visual Words, VLAD → image retrieval |
| **matching** | nearest descriptor ($L_1/L_2$), non-maximal suppression, or a trained classifier |
| **uses** | stereo correspondence → $F$/homography; object/pedestrian/character detection; image search |

### describe a whole image by its "visual words" (BoVW) or their residuals (VLAD) — and you can search by picture.

K-means codebook → K-bin histogram (BoVW) or K×D normalized residuals (VLAD); nearest-descriptor retrieval = CBIR.

### Self-check quiz

1. How is a Bag-of-Visual-Words dictionary built, and what does an image become?

→ Answer: K-means-cluster all library descriptors → $K$ visual words; an image → a $K$-bin histogram of nearest-word counts

2. What does VLAD store that BoVW discards, and what's its dimension?

→ Answer: the residuals $\sum(\mathbf x-C_i)$ (not just counts); dimension $K\times D$, then L2-normalized

3. What is content-based image retrieval?

→ Answer: search-by-image: match a query image's global descriptor to a library's descriptors and rank the closest

4. Name one use of keypoint descriptors that ties this topic back to earlier weeks.

→ Answer: automatically finding corresponding points across views → estimating the fundamental matrix / homography (Weeks 2, 4–5)

— end of Lecture 28 · end of Week 6 · end of the course 🎓✏️
